On 5 August 1975 the [Martin Marietta X-24B][ref_x24] came down the approach at Edwards Air Force Base at three hundred miles an hour, descending ten thousand feet a minute, with no engine running, and landed on the concrete. **It was not the first unpowered landing at Edwards and it was not the fastest. It was the first one that had to be precise**, and it removed a decision that had already been made about the Space Shuttle. This article is the twenty-fifth in the [X-Planes series][related_post_a297_xplanes_framing], following the [X-1][related_post_a298_bell_x1], the [X-2][related_post_a299_bell_x2], the [X-3][related_post_a300_douglas_x3], the [X-4][related_post_a301_northrop_x4], the [X-5][related_post_a302_bell_x5], the [X-6][related_post_a303_convair_x6], the [X-7][related_post_a304_lockheed_x7], the [X-8][related_post_a305_aerojet_x8], the [X-9][related_post_a306_bell_x9], the [X-10][related_post_a307_north_american_x10], the [X-11][related_post_a308_convair_x11], the [X-12][related_post_a309_convair_x12], the [X-13][related_post_a310_ryan_x13], the [X-14][related_post_a311_bell_x14], the [X-15][related_post_a312_north_american_x15], the [X-16][related_post_a313_bell_x16], the [X-17][related_post_a314_lockheed_x17], the [X-18][related_post_a315_hiller_x18], the [X-19][related_post_a316_curtiss_wright_x19], the [X-20][related_post_a317_boeing_x20], the [X-21][related_post_a318_northrop_x21], the [X-22][related_post_a319_bell_x22], and the [X-23][related_post_a320_martin_marietta_x23].

**This aircraft and the last one are the same shape and almost nothing else.** The [X-23][related_post_a320_martin_marietta_x23] was the unmanned SV-5D, thrown at the Pacific from an Atlas and asked whether a returning vehicle could steer. The X-24A is the piloted SV-5P, air-launched from a B-52 and asked whether a returning vehicle could be landed. **It is also the vehicle that was refused the designation X-23A in December 1965 and received X-24A in 1967**, which is the paperwork the previous article is about.

## The Research Question

**Can a pilot land a vehicle with a lift-to-drag ratio of three, without an engine, on a runway, on purpose?**

Every part of that sentence is doing work.

**Without an engine** means the approach is flown once. There is no go-around, no second attempt, and no way to add energy. Whatever the vehicle has when it starts down is all it will ever have.

**With a lift-to-drag ratio of three** means it descends at roughly eighteen degrees where a transport descends at three. The approach is not a glide in any sense a pilot of the period would have recognised. It is closer to a controlled fall.

**On a runway** is the part that was genuinely open. The period had been studying the problem since the late 1950s, when it became clear that a returning spacecraft would have to land somehow, in [Evan Kawamura et al, Simulated Vision-based Approach Lifting bodies had been landing on the dry lakebed at Edwards since 1963, and a lakebed is seven miles long and forgiving in every direction. **A concrete runway is fifteen thousand feet long and one hundred and fifty wide.**

The programme's answer, at the very end, was two landings on Edwards main runway by [John Manke and Michael Love][ref_nasa_x24b], and the consequence was that the Space Shuttle flew without the air-breathing engines it had been designed to carry for exactly this problem.

## Programme Origin

The X-24A was the piloted half of the Martin SV-5 family, the unpowered entry half of which is the subject of the [previous article][related_post_a320_martin_marietta_x23]. It joined a lifting-body programme already running at the NASA Flight Research Center with the M2 and HL-10 shapes, in [Koenig and Brady 1960][research_koenig_brady_1960], [Eldredge et al 1965][research_eldredge_1965], [Smith 1965][research_smith_1965], [Thompson 1966][research_thompson_1966], [Kock and Painter 1968][research_kock_painter_1968], [Layton and Mctigue 1969][research_layton_mctigue_1969], [Garrison P Layton 1970][research_garrison_p_layton_1970], [John A Manke et al 1970][research_john_a_manke_1970], [John G McTigue 1970][research_john_g_mctigue_1970], [Jon S Pyle and Lawrence G Ash 1970][research_jon_s_pyle_lawrence_g_ash_1970], [Ming H Tang 1970][research_ming_h_tang_1970], [Robert W Kempel et al 1970][research_robert_w_kempel_1970], [Thompson 1970][research_thompson_1970], [William H Dana and J R Gentry 1970][research_william_h_dana_j_r_gentry_1970], [Kempel and Thompson 1971][research_kempel_thompson_1971], [Kempel et al 1971][research_kempel_1971], [Pyle 1971][research_pyle_1971], [Tang 1971][research_tang_1971], [Tang and Pearson 1971][research_tang_pearson_1971], [Gentry 1972][research_gentry_1972], [Sim 1973, Flight-determined stability and c][research_sim_1973], [Kempel and Manke 1974][research_kempel_manke_1974], [Painter and Sitterle 1974][research_painter_sitterle_1974], [Kempel et al 1975][research_kempel_1975], [Manke and Love 1975][research_manke_love_1975], [Nagy and Kirsten 1976][research_nagy_kirsten_1976], [Armstrong 1977][research_armstrong_1977], [Kempel and Painter 1993][research_kempel_painter_1993], [R Dale Reed et al 1997][research_r_dale_reed_1997], [Barret 1998][research_barret_1998]. The programme's own narrative history is [Reed's account of the lifting bodies][book_reed_1997], written by a participant.

**The programme's logic was that the shape was the hard part and the speed was not.** A lifting body's entry aerodynamics could be measured by throwing small unmanned copies at the atmosphere, which is what PRIME did. Its landing aerodynamics could not, because landing requires a pilot and a pilot requires a vehicle large enough to hold one. So the same shape was built twice at different sizes and asked different questions.

The X-24A flew 28 times between April 1969 and June 1971, reaching Mach 1.6 and 71,400 feet. It was then returned to Martin Marietta and rebuilt. **The X-24B that emerged shared the X-24A's internal structure and almost none of its outer shape**, having been given a flat bottom, a double delta planform and a long pointed nose. It flew 36 times between August 1973 and November 1975.

Getting either of them airborne required a carrier aircraft and a rocket, in [Kansas Univ Lawrence 1952][research_kansas_univ_lawrence_1952], [Miele 1955][research_miele_1955], [Cicala and Miele 1956][research_cicala_miele_1956], [Miele 1957][research_miele_1957], [Brant and Hage 1964][research_brant_hage_1964], [Dempster and Bell 1965][research_dempster_bell_1965], [Burris and Bender 1969, Aircraft Load Alleviation and Mod][research_burris_bender_1969_2], [Ko and Schuster 1983][research_ko_schuster_1983], [Ko and Schuster 1984][research_ko_schuster_1984], [Whitmore et al 1994][research_whitmore_1994].

## The Vehicles

The dimensions matter here because the whole analysis runs on them, and **the accessible sources disagree in a way worth naming.** Several give the X-24A and the X-24B identical dimensions, which cannot be right for two vehicles one of which was rebuilt to be half again as long. The figures used here are from the flight-determined table in [Saltzman et al 2007][research_saltzman_2007], which is the source this article's aerodynamics rest on.

| Quantity | X-24A | X-24B |
|---|---|---|
| Length | 24.50 ft | 37.50 ft |
| Span | 13.63 ft | 19.14 ft |
| Reference area | 195.0 sq ft | 330.5 sq ft |
| Wetted area | 590.0 sq ft | 948.4 sq ft |
| Base area | 11.78 to 25.36 sq ft | 18.79 to 38.05 sq ft |
| Weight in the lift and drag tests | 6,360 lb | 8,500 lb |
| Aspect ratio | 0.953 | 1.108 |

**The base area is quoted as a range because it was not fixed.** The upper flaps that trimmed the vehicle also changed how much of the blunt rear end was exposed, so the drag the vehicle carried depended on how it was trimmed. That is not a detail. It is the subject of the next section.

## Sizing From First Principles

### Why a Reentry Vehicle Must Be Cut Off Flat

An aeroplane designed for efficiency ends in a point, because a body that closes smoothly leaves the air behind it at something close to the pressure it started at. **A reentry vehicle cannot do that.** The heat shield has to terminate somewhere, the rocket engine has to exhaust somewhere, and the structure has to be blunt enough not to melt. The result is a large flat base, and behind that base sits a region of separated flow at less than ambient pressure, pulling backwards. Written as a coefficient on the wetted area, that suction is

$$C_{D_b} = -C_{p_b}\,\frac{A_b}{A_w}$$

with the base pressure coefficient negative, so the term is a positive drag. **The whole of what follows is a fight with that one expression.**

The literature of that penalty is old and large, in [Hoerner 1950][research_hoerner_1950], [Chapman 1951][research_chapman_1951], [Chapman and Perkins 1951][research_chapman_perkins_1951], [Donaldson ### Hoerner's Relation, and How Wrong It Is at Full Scale

The base pressure is not independent of the rest of the vehicle, and the correlation used here comes from [Hoerner's compendium of drag data][book_hoerner_1965], which is where most of this subject's engineering practice still originates. A thicker boundary layer arriving at the base fills the wake and raises the pressure there, so **base drag falls as forebody drag rises**. Hoerner's correlation captures that inversely,

$$C_{p_b} = -\frac{K}{\sqrt{C_{D_\text{fore}}}}$$

with the forebody drag referred to the base area. **The coefficient is where the trouble is.** Hoerner obtained $K = 0.029$ from small-scale model data. Saltzman, Wang and Iliff, working from flight measurements on the X-15, the M2-F3 and the Space Shuttle, found that full-scale vehicles require

$$K = 0.090 \ \text{to}\ 0.100$$

$$\frac{0.100}{0.029} = 3.4$$

**Model-scale data understates the base drag of a real vehicle by a factor of about three and a half.**

**The article should say why, because the reason is not mysterious.** Base pressure is set by the boundary layer arriving at the base, and boundary layers are set by Reynolds number,

$$Re = \frac{V\ell}{\nu}$$

The X-24B on its approach flew at $Re = 9.8\times 10^7$ on its own length. A one-twentieth-scale model in a tunnel at 200 feet per second sits near $2.4\times 10^6$,

$$\frac{Re_\text{flight}}{Re_\text{model}} = 41$$

**A factor of forty in Reynolds number is not a small extrapolation**, and a correlation calibrated at the bottom of that range should not be expected to hold at the top of it. The general problem of what a model does and does not tell you about an aeroplane is [Sarah L Langston et al, Reynolds Number Effects on a 5.2%][research_sarah_l_langston], [Sarah L Langston et al, For a configuration whose base is a substantial fraction of its wetted area, that is not a correction. It is the difference between a vehicle that lands and one that does not.

### The Drag Bucket, and a Factor of Three That Depends on Nothing

Put the two together. The bookkeeping needs one definition the article has so far used without stating, the equivalent skin-friction coefficient, which is the minimum drag referred to wetted area rather than to a reference area nobody agrees on,

$$C_{F_e} = C_{D_\text{min}}\,\frac{S}{A_w}$$

and the forebody drag as Hoerner's relation wants it, referred to the base area,

$$C_{D_\text{fore},b} = C_{F_e,\text{fore}}\,\frac{A_w}{A_b}$$

Writing everything as an equivalent skin-friction coefficient referred to wetted area, with $r = A_b/A_w$ the ratio of base area to wetted area and $x$ the forebody contribution, the total is

$$C_{F_e} = x + \frac{k\, r^{3/2}}{\sqrt{x}}$$

where $k$ collects the Hoerner coefficient and a profile factor. **The two terms pull in opposite directions**, so there is an interior minimum. Differentiating,

$$\frac{dC_{F_e}}{dx} = 1 - \frac{k\,r^{3/2}}{2x^{3/2}} = 0 \quad \Longrightarrow \quad k\,r^{3/2} = 2x^{3/2}$$

which locates the optimum itself,

$$x_\text{opt} = \left(\frac{k}{2}\right)^{2/3} r$$

and substituting that back into the total gives something remarkable,

$$C_{F_e} = x + \frac{2x^{3/2}}{\sqrt{x}} = x + 2x = 3x$$

**At the optimum the total drag is exactly three times the forebody drag, and the base contributes exactly twice it.** The result contains no Hoerner coefficient, no profile factor and no area ratio. It is a property of the shape of the trade rather than of any vehicle.

The source's own two coefficients provide the check. It quotes the optimal forebody value as $0.1284\,r$ and the resulting minimum total as $0.3852\,r$, and

$$\frac{0.3852}{0.1284} = 3.000$$

**This article uses the source's absolute coefficients rather than its own.** The factor of three is parameter-free and is derived above, but the absolute value depends on exactly how the base pressure profile factor is applied, and the arrangement used here gives

$$\left(\frac{0.10/0.92}{2}\right)^{2/3} = 0.1435$$

where the source gives 0.1284, a difference of twelve percent. The structure is derived and the numbers are flight-anchored.

### What That Costs

Maximum lift-to-drag ratio for a given parasite drag and aspect ratio is standard,

$$\left(\frac{L}{D}\right)_\text{max} = \frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\pi e A}{C_{D_0}}}$$

and something useful happens when the drag is expressed on wetted area. Substituting $C_{D_0} = C_{F_e} A_w/S$ and $A = b^2/S$, **the reference area cancels completely**,

$$\left(\frac{L}{D}\right)_\text{max} = \frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\pi e\, b^2}{A_w\, C_{F_e}}}$$

That is why the right independent variable for this class of vehicle is span squared over **wetted** area, a quantity the source calls the wetted aspect ratio,

$$A = \frac{b^2}{S}, \qquad A_\text{wetted} = \frac{b^2}{A_w}$$

It removes the argument about what the reference area should be, which for a shape with no wing is not obvious.

Evaluating at the drag bucket, with an efficiency factor of 0.6,

| Vehicle | $b^2/A_w$ | Lift-to-drag ratio, clean base | Lift-to-drag ratio, full base |
|---|---|---|---|
| M2-F1 | 0.466 | 2.82 | 2.82 |
| M2-F2 | 0.216 | 2.32 | 2.32 |
| HL-10 | 0.402 | 3.91 | 2.79 |
| X-24A | 0.315 | 4.39 | 2.99 |
| X-24B | 0.386 | 4.88 | 3.43 |
| X-15 | 0.422 | 4.31 | 4.31 |
| Shuttle prototype | 0.515 | 4.07 | 4.07 |

**Three, near enough, for a vehicle whose shape was chosen to survive Mach 25.**

### A Check the Model Was Not Fitted To

The Space Shuttle prototype Enterprise flew in two configurations during its approach and landing tests, truncated and with a tailcone fitted over the blunt base. **That is the same airframe with and without the penalty**, which is as close to a controlled experiment as this subject offers.

With the tailcone the base drag is gone and only the forebody term survives, so the prediction is the same expression evaluated at $0.1284\,r$ rather than $0.3852\,r$. Since the ratio of those is three and the lift-to-drag ratio goes as the inverse square root of the drag,

$$\frac{(L/D)_\text{tailcone}}{(L/D)_\text{truncated}} = \sqrt{3} = 1.73$$

$$\left(\frac{L}{D}\right)_\text{tailcone} = 7.05$$

against a measured 7.5, an error of six percent. **Nothing in that prediction was fitted to the Enterprise.** It comes from the vehicle's span, its wetted area, its base area, and a factor of three derived from a differentiation.

**Covering the base is worth a factor of the square root of three in lift-to-drag ratio.** That is the price of being a reentry vehicle, stated as cleanly as this subject allows.

## Dependent Systems

### The Approach, Flown Fast and Steep on Purpose

Everything from here on runs off one relation the article has so far leaned on without displaying, the parabolic drag polar,

$$C_D = C_{D_0} + k_i C_L^2, \qquad k_i = \frac{1}{\pi e A} = 0.479$$

The second term is the drag that lift costs, and for a shape of aspect ratio near one it is neither small nor well described by the classical elliptic result, in [Sibert 1943][research_sibert_1943], [Polhamus 1953][research_polhamus_1953], [Graham et al 1954][research_graham_1954], [Nonweiler 1960][research_nonweiler_1960], [Robert S. Osborne and Thomas C. Kelly 1960][research_robert_s_osborne_thomas_c_kelly_1960], [Lakshminarayana 1962][research_lakshminarayana_1962], [Frost and Rutherford 1963][research_frost_rutherford_1963], [Sanders 1965][research_sanders_1965], [Gardner and Weir 1966][research_gardner_weir_1966], [Henderson 1966][research_henderson_1966], [Lundry 1967][research_lundry_1967], [Polhamus 1968][research_polhamus_1968], [Ward 1968][research_ward_1968], [Loth and Boyle 1969][research_loth_boyle_1969], [Snyder and Zumwalt 1969][research_snyder_zumwalt_1969], [Hancock 1972][research_hancock_1972], [Yajnik and Subbaiah 1976][research_yajnik_subbaiah_1976], [Haftka 1977][research_haftka_1977], [Lundry 1977][research_lundry_1977], [Laitone 1978, Positive Tail Loads for Minimum I][research_laitone_1978], [Deyoung 1980][research_deyoung_1980], [Sachs 1980][research_sachs_1980], [Axelson and Hill 1981][research_axelson_hill_1981], [Knaus 1983][research_knaus_1983], [Lottati 1984][research_lottati_1984], [Stark 1985][research_stark_1985], [Greene 1988][research_greene_1988], [Cole and Malmuth 1989][research_cole_malmuth_1989], [Greene 1989][research_greene_1989], [McCutchen 1989][research_mccutchen_1989].

together with the lift balance that fixes the lift coefficient at any given speed,

$$C_L = \frac{W}{q S}, \qquad q = \tfrac{1}{2}\rho V^2$$

The best glide condition is where the two drag terms are equal,

$$C_L^* = \sqrt{\frac{C_{D_0}}{k_i}} = 0.304, \qquad \left(\frac{L}{D}\right)_\text{max} = \frac{1}{2\sqrt{C_{D_0} k_i}} = 3.43$$

and the glide angle follows directly from the ratio,

$$\gamma^* = \arctan\frac{1}{(L/D)_\text{max}} = 16.2^\circ$$

At that condition the speed, from the ordinary lift balance, is

$$V^* = \sqrt{\frac{2W}{\rho S C_L^*}} = 276\ \text{ft/s} = 188\ \text{mph}$$

using the density at Edwards, which sits at 2,302 feet. **Every altitude and every dynamic pressure in this article rests on a tabulated atmosphere rather than on a measurement**, and the tabulation is a substantial piece of twentieth-century engineering in its own right, in [Webster 1947][research_webster_1947], [Standard Atmosphere - Tables 1955][research_standard_atmosphere_1955], [The International Civil Aviation Organization Standard Atmosphere 1957][research_the_international_1957], [Sterne 1958, Errata High-Altitude Atmospheric][research_sterne_1958_2], [Sterne 1958, High-Altitude Atmospheric Density][research_sterne_1958], [Pisharoty 1959][research_pisharoty_1959], [58. U. S. Standard Atmosphere, 1962 1963][research_b_34_u_1963], [U. S. Standard Atmosphere 1963][research_u_s_1963], [King-Hele 1964][research_king_hele_1964], [U.S. Standard Atmosphere Supplements 1966, U.S. Standard Atmosphere Suppleme][research_u_s_standard_1966], [U.S. standard atmosphere supplements 1966, U.S. standard atmosphere suppleme][research_u_s_standard_1966_2], [Minzner 1967][research_minzner_1967], [Srivastava 1967][research_srivastava_1967], [Ghosh 1970][research_ghosh_1970], [Karr 1972][research_karr_1972], [Minzner et al 1976][research_minzner_1976], [Peters and Jr 1977][research_peters_jr_1977], [Bass 1980][research_bass_1980], [Blish 1980][research_blish_1980], [Dennis P. Dykstra 1980][research_dennis_p_dykstra_1980], [Sharma 1980][research_sharma_1980], [Price and Blanchard 1981][research_price_blanchard_1981], [Other publications Reference/standard atmosphere 1987][research_other_publications_1987], [Zuppardo 1993][research_zuppardo_1993]. **The X-24B did not approach at 188 miles an hour. It approached at 300.**

That is sixty percent faster than its best glide,

$$\frac{V}{V^*} = 1.60$$

and flying faster than best glide makes the glide angle steeper, not shallower. **The polar says why.** Lift coefficient falls as the inverse square of speed, so at 1.60 times the best-glide speed it drops to

$$\frac{C_L}{C_L^*} = \left(\frac{V}{V^*}\right)^{-2} = 0.39$$

which lands at $C_L = 0.12$. **At that lift coefficient the induced term has almost vanished and the parasite term has not**,

$$\frac{k_i C_L^2}{C_D} = 0.13$$

so seven eighths of the drag is now the base and the skin, being paid for at a dynamic pressure of 215 pounds per square foot. The lift-to-drag ratio falls to 2.34, and

$$\gamma = \arctan\frac{1}{2.34} = 23.2^\circ$$

$$\dot h = V\sin\gamma = 173\ \text{ft/s} = 10{,}400\ \text{ft/min}$$

A transport on a three-degree approach at 140 knots descends at

$$\dot h = 140 \times 1.688 \times \sin 3^\circ \times 60 = 742\ \text{ft/min}$$

$$\frac{10{,}400}{742} = 14$$

**Ten thousand four hundred feet per minute, fourteen times a transport aircraft's approach.** The approach was deliberately made worse, and the reason is the next section.

### The Flare

The vehicle arrives at the ground with a great deal of speed and a sink rate that would destroy it. **The flare is the manoeuvre that converts one into the other**, and it is the whole problem, in [Pasek and Shanahan 1954][research_pasek_shanahan_1954], [Matranga 1961, Analysis of X-15 Landing Approach][research_matranga_1961], [Acton 1962][research_acton_1962], [Gannett and Mc Lean 1964][research_gannett_mc_lean_1964], [Chipman 1965][research_chipman_1965], [McDermott 1967][research_mcdermott_1967], [Waite et al 1967][research_waite_1967], [Waite and Arikawa 1968][research_waite_arikawa_1968], [Richardson 1969][research_richardson_1969], [Addy 1970][research_addy_1970], [Evanoff 1971][research_evanoff_1971], [Rubin 1971][research_rubin_1971], [Herkes 1972][research_herkes_1972], [Vavilov 1972][research_vavilov_1972], [Ohta and Sugiura 1973][research_ohta_sugiura_1973], [Renfroe 1973][research_renfroe_1973], [Douda 1974][research_douda_1974], [Leland and Thompson 1975][research_leland_thompson_1975], [Palmer and Petitt 1977][research_palmer_petitt_1977], [Papagiannis and Wefer 1978][research_papagiannis_wefer_1978], [Uchida 1980][research_uchida_1980], [Heffley and Schulman 1981][research_heffley_schulman_1981], [Nadkarni 1981][research_nadkarni_1981], [Walker 1981][research_walker_1981], [Watson et al 1983][research_watson_1983], [Bai 1987][research_bai_1987], [Chang and Muirhead 1987][research_chang_muirhead_1987], [Hynes et al 1988][research_hynes_1988], [Barnes 2009][research_barnes_2009].

Treating the flare as an arc that rotates the flight path from $-\gamma$ to zero at load factor $n$, the instantaneous radius is

$$R = \frac{V^2}{g(n-1)}$$

**That radius is not constant, and assuming it is gets the answer wrong.** The vehicle decelerates through the manoeuvre, so the arc tightens as it goes. Evaluating the radius once at the entry speed makes the path too long, which overstates the work done against drag, which understates the touchdown speed. The error is nine percent in height and twelve percent in speed at moderate load factor, and at low load factor it is qualitative. **The flare below is therefore integrated rather than assumed circular.** Stepping in path angle, with $s$ the distance along the path, the system is

$$\frac{ds}{d\gamma} = -R = -\frac{V^2}{g(n-1)}$$

$$\frac{dh}{ds} = \sin\gamma, \qquad \frac{dx}{ds} = \cos\gamma$$

$$\frac{dV}{ds} = \frac{W\sin\gamma - D}{mV}$$

with the drag evaluated at the instantaneous speed and at the lift coefficient the load factor demands,

$$C_L = \frac{nW}{qS}, \qquad D = qS\left(C_{D_0} + k_i C_L^2\right)$$

**Three equations and a polar.** The first is what makes the arc tighten, because $R$ carries $V^2$ and $V$ is falling throughout.

The load factor is not assumed either. It is defined by

$$n = \frac{L}{W}$$

and it is bounded by structure and by the pilot as well as by aerodynamics, in [Effect of Variations in weight or Normal Acceleration on the Ceiling of Jet–Propelled Aircraft — Correspondence 1955][research_effect_of_1955], [Sutcliffe 1955][research_sutcliffe_1955], [Mayer and Hamer 1961][research_mayer_hamer_1961], [Roth 1969][research_roth_1969], [White 1971][research_white_1971], [Anderson et al 1973][research_anderson_1973], [Keskar and Slater 1979][research_keskar_slater_1979], [Godfrey 1991][research_godfrey_1991], [Thornton 1993][research_thornton_1993]. **The touchdown speed is quoted, so the load factor can be solved for**, which converts a guess into a statement about what was flown. Integrating the system above and adjusting $n$ until the vehicle reaches the runway at 200 miles an hour gives

$$n = 1.19, \qquad h_\text{flare} = 2{,}163\ \text{ft}, \qquad t = 21.9\ \text{s}$$

**Two thousand one hundred and sixty feet, and twenty-two seconds.** A transport aircraft begins its flare at about thirty feet and is done in three,

$$\frac{2{,}163}{30} = 72, \qquad \frac{21.9}{3} = 7.3$$

**The X-24B began its flare seventy times higher and stayed in it seven times longer.** The arc is 9,616 feet and its horizontal projection is 9,306, so during the flare the vehicle covers

$$\frac{9{,}306}{15{,}000} = 0.62$$

of the length of the concrete runway it is aiming at. **The manoeuvre is longer than most of the runway.**

The lift coefficient the flare demands is modest, which is worth checking rather than assuming,

$$C_L = \frac{nW}{qS} = 1.19 \times 0.120 = 0.142$$

against a maximum usable value near 0.7, so **the vehicle is nowhere near stall when the flare begins**. It becomes so only at the very end, which is the subject of the corridor.

### The Flare Has a Corridor

**The obvious way to touch down slower is to pull less, and that does work, but only within a band.**

| Load factor | Flare begins at | Arc | Time | Touchdown speed |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1.05 g | 6,780 ft | 25,989 ft | 59.1 s | 47 mph |
| 1.10 g | 3,804 ft | 15,985 ft | 36.3 s | 154 mph |
| 1.19 g | 2,163 ft | 9,616 ft | 21.9 s | 199 mph |
| 1.30 g | 1,425 ft | 6,520 ft | 14.8 s | 222 mph |
| 1.50 g | 880 ft | 4,124 ft | 9.4 s | 241 mph |
| 2.00 g | 449 ft | 2,141 ft | 4.9 s | 256 mph |
| 3.00 g | 224 ft | 1,073 ft | 2.4 s | 260 mph |

**The band is bounded from both ends and neither bound is a matter of comfort.**

At the top, pulling harder buys almost nothing,

$$V_\text{td}(3g) - V_\text{td}(2g) = 260 - 256 = 4\ \text{mph}$$

because a tighter arc is a shorter arc and spends less energy against drag. **The vehicle arrives fast however hard it is pulled.**

At the bottom, a gentle flare is a very long one. At 1.10 g the arc is nearly 16,000 feet and takes 36 seconds, and by 1.05 g the vehicle has decayed to 47 miles an hour, which is far below any speed at which this shape flies. **The maximum usable lift coefficient is itself an assumption and a contested one for this class of shape**, since a lifting body does not stall in the way a wing does, in [Graham and Luskin 1946][research_graham_luskin_1946], [Nonweiler 1955, Maximum Lift Data for Symmetrical][research_nonweiler_1955], [The Pursuit of Maximum Lift 1955][research_the_pursuit_1955], [Vidal et al 1961][research_vidal_1961], [Wannenburg 1963][research_wannenburg_1963], [Conner et al 1965][research_conner_1965], [Lusty and Miele 1966][research_lusty_miele_1966], [Maikapar 1966][research_maikapar_1966], [Wootton 1967][research_wootton_1967], [Huang 1968][research_huang_1968], [Miele and Damoulakis 1969][research_miele_damoulakis_1969], [Pritchard 1969][research_pritchard_1969], [Bridson and Hancock 1970][research_bridson_hancock_1970], [Liebeck and Ormsbee 1970][research_liebeck_ormsbee_1970], [Thompson and Hull 1970][research_thompson_hull_1970], [Ormsbee and Chen 1972][research_ormsbee_chen_1972], [Bihrle and Bowman 1981][research_bihrle_bowman_1981], [Lapygin and Tret'yakov 1986][research_lapygin_tret_yakov_1986], [Deese and Agarwal 1990][research_deese_agarwal_1990]. Taking a value of 0.7, the stall speed is

$$V_\text{stall} = \sqrt{\frac{2W}{\rho S C_{L_\text{max}}}} = 124\ \text{mph}$$

and the flare reaches it at about 1.07 g. At the solved 1.19 g the margin is

$$\frac{V_\text{td}}{V_\text{stall}} = \frac{199}{124} = 1.60$$

**The usable corridor is therefore roughly 1.07 g to 1.5 g, about four tenths of a g wide**, with stalling short of the runway at one end and arriving at 240 miles an hour at the other.

**That corridor is not this article's invention.** It is what [Walker 1981][research_walker_1981] describes when it speaks of boundaries between marginal and routine and of an optimal preflare condition at the centre of them, and it says explicitly that those boundaries rest on judgements formed from X-15 and lifting-body experience.

### What the Landing Gear Sees

**The article has so far said nothing about the number a landing-gear engineer would ask for first**, which is the sink rate at the moment of contact.

In this model the flare drives the path angle to zero, so the sink rate goes to zero with it and the honest way to report the answer is at a small residual angle. Integrating the same system and stopping short,

$$\dot h = V\sin\gamma_\text{residual}$$

| Residual path angle | Speed | Sink rate |
|---|---|---|
| 2.0 degrees | 211 mph | 10.8 ft/s |
| 1.0 degrees | 205 mph | 5.3 ft/s |
| 0.5 degrees | 202 mph | 2.6 ft/s |

Designing a gear to absorb that, and knowing what it will actually see, is a subject with its own long literature, in [Walker 1945][research_walker_1945], [Brown and Redwine **A transport touches down at about 2 feet per second and the X-15's gear was designed for 9.** The X-24B therefore had to arrive within about half a degree of level to land as gently as an airliner, and within two degrees to stay inside a research aircraft's design case. **That is the precision the corridor is really about**, and it is a tighter statement of the problem than the touchdown speed alone.

### Energy, Spent Exactly Once

The reason none of this can be retried is worth making quantitative. Expressing the vehicle's total energy at the start of the flare as the height it would be worth,

$$h_E = h + \frac{V^2}{2g} = 2{,}163 + 3{,}009 = 5{,}172\ \text{ft}$$

At touchdown the vehicle has no height left and only its speed, so

$$h_{E,\text{td}} = \frac{V_\text{td}^2}{2g} = 1{,}322\ \text{ft}$$

$$\frac{5{,}172 - 1{,}322}{5{,}172} = 0.74$$

**Seventy-four percent of everything the vehicle has left is spent in the flare.** An earlier version of this calculation multiplied a constant approach drag along the whole arc and obtained 83 percent, which overstates it, because the drag falls with the speed. The difference of energy heights needs no such assumption. The mean deceleration along the arc is

$$\bar a = \frac{V_i^2 - V_f^2}{2 s} = 0.18\,g$$

The literature of managing that quantity is [Gerdes and Hynes 1971][research_gerdes_hynes_1971], [Hardy 1972][research_hardy_1972], [Sederstrom et al 1973][research_sederstrom_1973], [Crane et al 1974][research_crane_1974], [Bull 1976][research_bull_1976], [Hawthorne 1976, Terminal area energy management r][research_hawthorne_1976], [Hawthorne 1976, Terminal area energy management r][research_hawthorne_1976_2], [Hawthorne 1976, Terminal area energy management r][research_hawthorne_1976_3], [Kraiss and Baty 1978][research_kraiss_baty_1978], [Halyo 1983][research_halyo_1983], [Day 2004][research_day_2004].

### What the Reshaping Bought

The X-24B was the X-24A's structure under a new outer shape, so the two make a controlled comparison of their own.

$$\left(\frac{L}{D}\right)_\text{X-24A} = 2.99, \qquad \left(\frac{L}{D}\right)_\text{X-24B} = 3.43, \qquad \text{ratio} = 1.15$$

**Almost all of that came from span rather than from a cleaner base.** The wetted aspect ratio rose by a factor of 1.23 while the base-to-wetted ratio barely moved, at 0.93 of its former value, and since the lift-to-drag ratio goes as the square root of the wetted aspect ratio,

$$\sqrt{1.23} = 1.11$$

which is most of the 1.15 observed. **The long pointed nose was buying span-loading, not base cleanliness**, which is the opposite of what the shape suggests at a glance.

### How Far It Could Reach

A glide at a fixed lift-to-drag ratio reaches a radius proportional to the height it starts from,

$$r = h\,\frac{L}{D}$$

which for the X-24B from 20,000 feet is 13 miles, and from 40,000 feet is 26. The performance of a glide considered as a range problem is [Bell 1964][research_bell_1964], [Knapp and Barton 1968][research_knapp_barton_1968], [Sleeman 1971][research_sleeman_1971], [Kelley et al 1982][research_kelley_1982]. **That is the entire manoeuvring room available**, and inside it the vehicle must arrive at a runway threshold at a particular speed on a particular heading, having spent the whole approach unable to add any energy at all.

## The Flight Test Record

| | X-24A | X-24B |
|---|---|---|
| First flight | 17 April 1969, unpowered | 1 August 1973, unpowered |
| First powered flight | 19 March 1970 | 15 November 1973 |
| Flights | 28 | 36 |
| Maximum speed | Mach 1.6, 1,036 mph | 1,164 mph |
| Maximum altitude | 71,400 ft | 74,130 ft |
| Last flight | June 1971 | 26 November 1975 |

**The two concrete-runway landings came at the end and were the point.** Every earlier lifting-body landing had been on Rogers Dry Lake, whose runway is about 39,000 feet long against the 15,000 feet of the Edwards concrete, a factor of 2.6 in the margin available for an error.

## Comparison With Ground Prediction

| Quantity | This model | Flight |
|---|---|---|
| X-24A maximum lift-to-drag ratio | 2.99 | about 2.9 reported |
| X-24B maximum lift-to-drag ratio | 3.43 to 4.88 | about 4.5 reported |
| Enterprise with tailcone | 7.05 | 7.5 measured |
| Approach sink rate | 10,400 ft/min | steep glides, not quoted numerically |
| Touchdown speed | 199 mph at a solved 1.19 g | about 200 mph, quoted |

**The model is good to within about eight percent everywhere it can be checked independently**, using nothing but published geometry, a Hoerner correlation with a flight-corrected coefficient, and a factor of three obtained by differentiation. The touchdown row is not an independent check, because the load factor was solved to reproduce it; what that row reports is the load factor, not an agreement.

The weakest link is the efficiency factor of 0.6, which is taken from the source's own comparison curve rather than derived. **It enters the lift-to-drag ratio as a square root**, so a twenty percent error in it moves the answer by ten percent, which is the same order as the agreement being claimed.

## What the Data Changed

**The Space Shuttle was designed to carry air-breathing engines for its landing approach, and it did not fly with them.**

The argument for them was straightforward. An orbiter that could go around, loiter, and be flown to a landing under power would be a far less demanding vehicle to operate, and its pilots could practise. The argument against was mass, and mass in an orbiter is payload, since every pound of engine and fuel is a pound not carried to orbit.

**What the lifting bodies supplied was evidence that the engines were not needed.** The X-24B's two precision landings on concrete demonstrated that a vehicle with a lift-to-drag ratio near four could be brought to a chosen point on a runway by a pilot with no thrust available. The consequence, in the words of the [programme's own account][ref_nasa_x24b], was that shuttle designers eliminated the auxiliary engines.

## Where the Framing Breaks Down

**Treating the X-24 through its landing gets three things wrong.**

**The vehicles were not landing demonstrators.** They were transonic and low-supersonic research aircraft that happened to land without engines because that is how they were flown. The X-24B reached Mach 1.5, and a great deal of the flight record is about stability and control at speeds that have nothing to do with the approach.

**The base drag analysis is retrospective.** Saltzman, Wang and Iliff published in the 1990s and 2000s, drawing on flight data gathered decades earlier. **Nobody sized the X-24B using the factor of three derived above.** The relation explains the vehicle rather than having produced it, and this article should not be read as a reconstruction of the design process.

**The flare corridor is computed from a point-mass energy model**, with a circular arc, a constant load factor and a drag polar that is a parabola. A real flare is none of those things. The agreement to eight percent is encouraging and is not a validation.

There is a fourth, about attribution. **Two concrete landings do not by themselves settle a design decision** as large as deleting an orbiter's engines. They were one input among many, and the article's account of the causal chain leans on a programme retrospective rather than on the decision record.

## The Contemporary Literature

**Everything the X-24B demonstrated is now done by software, and the one thing that has not moved is the corridor, because the corridor is set by energy rather than by skill.**

There is a second answer as well, and it is the more radical one. **The X-24's entire difficulty was that no energy could be added after the approach began.** A booster that lands on its engine answers that by adding energy, which is not a better unpowered landing but a decision not to attempt one. **Both answers are in service and they are opposites.**

**The milestone came thirteen years after the X-24B.** On 15 November 1988 the Soviet orbiter Buran flew one uncrewed orbital mission and landed itself on the runway at Baikonur, unpowered, in a crosswind, with no pilot aboard at any point. **That is the same demonstration Manke and Love flew, performed by a machine, at fifty times the weight.**

**What the automation did not do is widen the corridor.** The bounds derived above are energy bounds and they do not care who is flying. Written out, the touchdown speed is a function

$$V_\text{td} = F\left(n,\ \frac{W}{S},\ C_{D_0},\ k_i,\ \rho\right)$$

in which **there is no term describing the pilot**. An autonomous vehicle arriving too fast still floats, and one arriving too slow still runs out of speed. What automation buys is consistency in hitting the middle of a corridor whose width is set by aerodynamics, in [Bottasso and Montinari 2015][research_bottasso_montinari_2015], [Ye et al 2015, Flight Envelope Protection Contro][research_ye_2015], [Zhou et al 2015][research_zhou_2015], [Ellerbroek et al 2016][research_ellerbroek_2016], [Ackerman et al 2017][research_ackerman_2017], [Lee et al 2017, L1 Adaptive Control Within a Flig][research_lee_2017], [Lombaerts et al 2017][research_lombaerts_2017], [Schuet et al 2017][research_schuet_2017], [Tekles et al 2017][research_tekles_2017], [Fellah and Guiatni 2019][research_fellah_guiatni_2019], [Gabrys et al 2019][research_gabrys_2019], [Zaal et al 2019][research_zaal_2019], [Van Baelen et al 2020][research_van_baelen_2020], [Campbell et al 2022][research_campbell_2022], [Kluever 2022][research_kluever_2022], [Yu et al 2022][research_yu_2022], [Ma and Chen 2023][research_ma_chen_2023], [Altunkaya et al 2024][research_altunkaya_2024], [Catak et al 2024][research_catak_2024], [Hsu et al 2024][research_hsu_2024], [Schieni et al 2024][research_schieni_2024], [Autenrieb 2025][research_autenrieb_2025], [Sarah et al 2025][research_sarah_2025], [Yue et al 2025][research_yue_2025].

The comparison is worth making precisely, because it is a trade and not a victory. The X-24B carried no landing propellant and paid for that with a corridor four tenths of a g wide and no second attempt. A booster that lands on its engine carries propellant it could otherwise have spent on payload, and buys a hover, a divert and an abort.

**Which is better depends entirely on what is coming back.** For a stage that is mostly empty tank the propellant is affordable. For a vehicle returning from orbit with a heat shield already sized for the entry, carrying landing propellant through that entry is expensive, which is why the vehicles that come back from orbit still land the X-24B's way and the ones that come back from a few minutes of flight do not.

2026][research_santho_2026].

A heavy goods vehicle is a blunt-based body whose drag is dominated by its wake, which is the X-24's problem in a different medium and at a lower speed. **One of the authors of the lifting-body base-drag analysis went on to apply the same treatment to heavy trucks**, which is as direct a statement of the connection as anyone could ask for. Projectile base bleed and boat-tailing belong to the same family.

**The reason the physics travelled is that the drag bucket does not care what the body is for.** Base drag falls as forebody drag rises whatever the body, so the factor of three at the optimum is as true of a lorry as of a lifting body.

### What the Survey Shows

**The aerodynamics have not moved.** A truncated base still costs a factor of the square root of three in lift-to-drag ratio, the flare still has a corridor bounded from both ends, and the energy is still spent exactly once.

**What moved is who flies it, and whether the problem is accepted at all.** The X-24B's pilots hit a fifteen-thousand-foot runway from a corridor a few tenths of a g wide, twice, and that was the demonstration. A modern vehicle of that class does the same thing on every flight and nobody writes it up. **And a modern booster refuses the problem entirely by carrying the propellant to add energy back**, which is the option the X-24B did not have and would not have wanted.

## The Source Base

The research literature here was assembled from the NASA Technical Reports Server, from Defense Technical Information Center records reached through their registered identifiers, and from the Crossref index, giving a pool of 1,674 records.

**The A320 pool was not imported**, which is the eighth consecutive article for that rule and the case where it was most tempting to break it. The two articles share a vehicle family and almost no literature, because entry mechanics at Mach 25 and unpowered landing at Mach 0.5 are different subjects with different vocabularies.

**The vocabulary of this subject belongs to other fields more than to aeronautics, and three cases are severe.** Flare is overwhelmingly the solar flare, and secondarily the gas flare and the flare stack of petrochemical engineering. Energy management belongs to power grids, buildings and batteries, and a pattern for it retrieved a manual on energy conservation in Navy family housing. Base is the air base, the database and the base station, and survives here only because base drag carries its own disambiguating word.

## Epistemic State

**Historical fact, as reported by the accessible record.** The X-24A flew 28 times between April 1969 and June 1971, reaching Mach 1.6 and 71,400 feet. It was rebuilt as the X-24B, which flew 36 times between August 1973 and November 1975, reaching 1,164 miles an hour and 74,130 feet. Two of the final X-24B flights landed on the main concrete runway at Edwards, flown by John Manke and Michael Love. The X-24A is the vehicle for which X-23A was requested in November 1965 and refused in December, and which received X-24A in 1967.

**Flight-determined data taken from a published source.** The dimensions, wetted areas, base areas and weights in the vehicle table are from Saltzman, Wang and Iliff, as is the finding that full-scale base drag requires a Hoerner coefficient near 0.10 against the model-scale 0.029, the pair of optimum coefficients 0.1284 and 0.3852, and the measured tailcone-on lift-to-drag ratio of 7.5 for the Enterprise.

**Engineering analysis, performed here and checkable.** The drag bucket and its differentiation, the result that total drag is three times forebody drag at the optimum, the cancellation of reference area from the lift-to-drag expression, the predicted lift-to-drag ratios for all seven vehicles, the square-root-of-three tailcone prediction, the approach angle and sink rate, the integrated flare, the load factor solved from the quoted touchdown speed, the corridor bounds, and the energy accounting are all derived in this article from stated assumptions.

**Inference, and flagged as such.** That the X-24B approached in a high-base-area trim is inferred from the fact that the resulting lift-to-drag ratio reproduces the observed touchdown speed, not from a stated flap setting. That the flare was flown at about 1.19 g is inferred by solving the integrated flare against the quoted touchdown speed, and it inherits every assumption in that model. That the two concrete landings caused the deletion of the orbiter's air-breathing engines follows a programme retrospective and is not established from the decision record.

**Assumptions that everything downstream inherits.** The Oswald efficiency factor of 0.6 is taken from the source's comparison curve. The flare load factor of 1.5 g is chosen, and the table shows what other choices give. The drag polar is parabolic, the flare is a circular arc at constant load factor, and the vehicle is a point mass throughout.

**What the record does not settle.** The actual trim and base area on the approach. The actual load factor flown in the flare. The touchdown speeds and sink rates of the two concrete landings, which were certainly recorded and are not in the accessible literature. And the weight of the X-24B's landings in the engine-deletion decision relative to the mass argument that was already against them.

## Out of Scope

The X-24A's transonic and supersonic flight record, which is most of its flights. The M2 and HL-10 programmes, which have their own histories and are covered in [Jenkins, Landis and Miller][book_jenkins_landis_miller_2003]. The XLR-11 engine, which the [X-1][related_post_a298_bell_x1] article covers. The Space Shuttle Approach and Landing Tests as a programme. Lifting-body structural design and the rebuild that turned the A into the B. The full decision record on the orbiter's air-breathing engines.

## Conclusion

**A blunt base costs a factor of the square root of three in lift-to-drag ratio, and everything difficult about landing an X-24 follows from that one number.**

Three instead of five means an eighteen-degree glide instead of eleven. It means approaching sixty percent faster than best glide, on purpose, because the flare needs the energy. It means beginning that flare a thousand feet up rather than thirty, and it means a corridor a few tenths of a g wide with running out of speed on one side and floating off the far end of the runway on the other.

**The X-24B flew through the middle of that corridor onto fifteen thousand feet of concrete, twice, and an orbiter that had been designed with jet engines for its landing approach flew without them.**
